sensitive data\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/admin/logs\" | \\\n  grep -iE \"(password|token|secret|credit_card|ssn)\" | head -10\n# Logs should not contain sensitive data in plaintext\n\n# Test for sensitive data in error messages\ncurl -s -X POST \\\n  -H \"Content-Type: application/json\" \\\n  -d '{\"email\":\"duplicate@test.com\"}' \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/register\"\n# Should not reveal: \"User with email duplicate@test.com already exists\"\n# Should show: \"Registration failed\" (generic)\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Concept | Description |\n|---------|-------------|\n| **Sensitive Data Exposure** | Unintended disclosure of PII, credentials, financial data, or health records |\n| **Data Over-Exposure** | API returning more data fields than the client needs |\n| **Secret Leakage** | API keys, tokens, or credentials exposed in client-side code or logs |\n| **Data at Rest** | Sensitive data stored in databases, files, or backups without encryption |\n| **Data in Transit** | Sensitive data transmitted over network without TLS encryption |\n| **Data Masking** | Replacing sensitive data with redacted values (e.g., showing last 4 digits of credit card) |\n| **PII** | Personally Identifiable Information - data that can identify an individual |\n| **Information Leakage** | Excessive error messages, stack traces, or debug information in responses |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n| Tool | Purpose |\n|------|---------|\n| **Burp Suite Professional** | Response analysis and regex-based sensitive data scanning |\n| **trufflehog** | Secret detection across git repos, filesystems, and cloud storage |\n| **gitleaks** | Git repository scanning for hardcoded secrets |\n| **testssl.sh** | TLS/SSL configuration assessment |\n| **git-dumper** | Downloading exposed .git directories from web servers |\n| **SecretFinder** | JavaScript file analysis for exposed API keys and tokens |\n| **Retire.js** | Detecting JavaScript libraries with known vulnerabilities |\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario 1: API Key in JavaScript Bundle\nThe application's JavaScript bundle contains a hardcoded Google Maps API key and a Stripe publishable key. The Stripe key has overly broad permissions, allowing the attacker to create charges.\n\n### Scenario 2: User API Returns Password Hashes\nThe `/api/users` endpoint returns complete user objects including bcrypt password hashes. Attackers can extract hashes and attempt offline cracking.\n\n### Scenario 3: PII in Cached API Responses\nThe user profile API endpoint returns full SSN and credit card numbers without masking. The endpoint does not set `Cache-Control: no-store`, so responses are cached in the browser and proxy caches.\n\n### Scenario 4: Git Repository with Database Credentials\nThe `.git` directory is accessible on the production server. Using git-dumper, the attacker downloads the repository history, finding database credentials committed in an early commit that were later \"removed\" but remain in git history.\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Sensitive Data Exposure Assessment Report\n\n**Target**: target.example.com\n**Assessment Date**: 2024-01-15\n**OWASP Category**: A02:2021 - Cryptographic Failures\n\n### Findings Summary\n| Finding | Severity | Data Type |\n|---------|----------|-----------|\n| API keys in JavaScript source | High | Credentials |\n| Password hashes in API response | Critical | Authentication |\n| Unmasked SSN in user profile | Critical | PII |\n| Credit card number in export | High | Financial |\n| .git directory exposed | Critical | Source code + secrets |\n| Missing TLS on API endpoint | High | All data in transit |\n| Sensitive data in error messages | Medium | Technical info |\n\n### Critical: Exposed Secrets\n| Secret Type | Location | Risk |\n|-------------|----------|------|\n| AWS Access Key (AKIA...) | /static/app.js line 342 | AWS resource access |\n| Stripe Secret Key (sk_live_...) | .env (via .git exposure) | Payment processing |\n| Database URL with credentials | .git history commit abc123 | Database access |\n| JWT Signing Secret | config.json (via .git) | Token forgery |\n\n### Data Over-Exposure in APIs\n| Endpoint | Unnecessary Fields Returned |\n|----------|-----------------------------|\n| GET /api/users | password_hash, internal_id, created_ip |\n| GET /api/users/{id} | ssn, credit_card_full, date_of_birth |\n| GET /api/orders | customer_phone, customer_address |\n\n### Recommendation\n1. Remove all hardcoded secrets from client-side code; use backend proxies\n2. Rotate all exposed credentials immediately\n3. Remove .git directory from production web root\n4. Implement response field filtering; return only required fields\n5. Mask sensitive data (SSN, credit card) in all API responses\n6. Add Cache-Control: no-store to all sensitive endpoints\n7. Enable TLS 1.2+ on all endpoints; redirect HTTP to HTTPS\n8. Implement secret scanning in CI/CD pipeline (trufflehog/gitleaks)\n```\n/root/.codex/skills/testing-for-sensitive-data-exposure/references/api-reference.md\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

{"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_25","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"sed -n '1,420p' /root/.codex/skills/exploiting-sql-injection-vulnerabilities/SKILL.md; find /root/.codex/skills/exploiting-sql-injection-vulnerabilities/references -maxdepth 1 -type f -print 2>/dev/null\"","aggregated_output":"---\nname: exploiting-sql-injection-vulnerabilities\ndescription: 'Identifies and exploits SQL injection vulnerabilities in web applications\n  during authorized penetration tests using manual techniques and automated tools\n  like sqlmap. The tester detects injection points through error-based, union-based,\n  blind boolean, and time-based blind techniques across all major database engines\n  (MySQL, PostgreSQL, MSSQL, Oracle) to demonstrate data extraction, authentication\n  bypass, and potential remote code execution. Activates for requests involving SQL\n  injection testing, SQLi exploitation, database security assessment, or injection\n  vulnerability verification.\n\n  '\ndomain: cybersecurity\nsubdomain: penetration-testing\ntags:\n- SQL-injection\n- sqlmap\n- database-security\n- OWASP-A03\n- injection-testing\nversion: 1.0.0\nauthor: mahipal\nlicense: Apache-2.0\nnist_csf:\n- ID.RA-01\n- ID.RA-06\n- GV.OV-02\n- DE.AE-07\nmitre_attack:\n- T1595\n- T1190\n- T1059\n- T1078\n- T1055\n---\n# Exploiting SQL Injection Vulnerabilities\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Testing web application input parameters for SQL injection vulnerabilities during an authorized penetration test\n- Validating that parameterized queries and input sanitization are properly implemented across all database interactions\n- Demonstrating the business impact of a confirmed SQL injection vulnerability by extracting sensitive data\n- Verifying that WAF rules and input validation controls effectively block SQL injection payloads\n- Testing stored procedures, dynamic SQL, and ORM bypass scenarios in enterprise applications\n\n**Do not use** against databases without written authorization, for extracting or exfiltrating actual customer data beyond what is needed for proof of concept, or against production databases where exploitation could corrupt data integrity.\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Written authorization specifying the target application and permissible level of exploitation (detection only vs. full exploitation)\n- Burp Suite Professional configured as an intercepting proxy to capture and modify HTTP requests\n- sqlmap installed with current version for automated detection and exploitation\n- Knowledge of the target database engine (MySQL, PostgreSQL, MSSQL, Oracle) or ability to fingerprint it\n- Test accounts at various privilege levels to test injection in authenticated contexts\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1: Injection Point Discovery\n\nIdentify parameters that interact with the database:\n\n- **Map all input vectors**: Catalog every parameter in URLs (GET), request bodies (POST), HTTP headers (Cookie, Referer, User-Agent, X-Forwarded-For), and JSON/XML API payloads\n- **Error-based detection**: Inject a single quote (`'`) into each parameter and observe the response. SQL errors (e.g., \"You have an error in your SQL syntax\", \"unterminated quoted string\", \"ORA-01756\") confirm the parameter reaches the database unsanitized.\n- **Boolean-based detection**: Inject `' AND 1=1--` (true condition) and `' AND 1=2--` (false condition). If the responses differ (different content length, different data returned, different HTTP status), the parameter is injectable.\n- **Time-based detection**: Inject `'; WAITFOR DELAY '0:0:5'--` (MSSQL), `' AND SLEEP(5)--` (MySQL), or `'; SELECT pg_sleep(5)--` (PostgreSQL). A 5-second response delay confirms injection.\n- **Out-of-band detection**: Use payloads that trigger DNS or HTTP requests to a Burp Collaborator domain to confirm injection in scenarios where responses are not directly observable.\n- **Second-order injection**: Test for injection where input is stored and later used in a different SQL query (e.g., username stored at registration, used in a query on the profile page).\n\n### Step 2: Database Fingerprinting\n\nDetermine the database engine and version to select appropriate exploitation techniques:\n\n- **Error-based fingerprinting**: Each database produces distinctive error messages. MySQL includes \"MySQL\", MSSQL mentions \"SQL Server\", PostgreSQL references \"PG\", Oracle contains \"ORA-\".\n- **Function-based fingerprinting**: Inject database-specific functions:\n  - MySQL: `' AND VERSION()--` or `' AND @@version--`\n  - MSSQL: `' AND @@version--` or `' AND DB_NAME()--`\n  - PostgreSQL: `' AND version()--`\n  - Oracle: `' AND banner FROM v$version--`\n- **String concatenation differences**: MySQL uses `CONCAT('a','b')` or `'a' 'b'`, MSSQL uses `'a'+'b'`, PostgreSQL uses `'a'||'b'`, Oracle uses `'a'||'b'`\n- **Comment syntax**: MySQL supports `#` and `-- `, MSSQL uses `-- `, PostgreSQL uses `-- `, Oracle uses `-- `\n\n### Step 3: Manual Exploitation Techniques\n\nExploit confirmed injection points using technique-appropriate methods:\n\n- **UNION-based extraction**: Determine the number of columns with `ORDER BY` incrementing (`' ORDER BY 1--`, `' ORDER BY 2--`, etc. until an error occurs). Then construct UNION SELECT to extract data:\n  ```\n  ' UNION SELECT NULL,username,password,NULL FROM users--\n  ```\n- **Error-based extraction** (MySQL): Use `EXTRACTVALUE` or `UPDATEXML` to force data into error messages:\n  ```\n  ' AND EXTRACTVALUE(1,CONCAT(0x7e,(SELECT @@version),0x7e))--\n  ```\n- **Blind boolean extraction**: Extract data one character at a time by testing character values:\n  ```\n  ' AND SUBSTRING((SELECT password FROM users WHERE username='admin'),1,1)='a'--\n  ```\n- **Time-based blind extraction**: Same character-by-character approach using time delays:\n  ```\n  ' AND IF(SUBSTRING((SELECT password FROM users WHERE username='admin'),1,1)='a',SLEEP(5),0)--\n  ```\n- **Stacked queries** (where supported): Execute additional SQL statements:\n  ```\n  '; INSERT INTO users(username,password,role) VALUES('attacker','password','admin')--\n  ```\n\n### Step 4: Automated Exploitation with sqlmap\n\nUse sqlmap for efficient exploitation of confirmed injection points:\n\n- **Basic detection**: `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/page?id=1\" --batch --random-agent` to detect injection and identify the database\n- **Extract databases**: `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/page?id=1\" --dbs` to list all databases\n- **Extract tables**: `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/page?id=1\" -D <database> --tables` to list tables\n- **Extract data**: `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/page?id=1\" -D <database> -T users --dump --threads 5` to extract table contents\n- **POST parameters**: `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/login\" --data=\"username=test&password=test\" -p username` to test POST parameters\n- **Cookie injection**: `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/page\" --cookie=\"session=abc123; id=1*\" --level 2` to test cookie parameters (mark injectable parameter with *)\n- **OS command execution** (if DB user has sufficient privileges): `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/page?id=1\" --os-shell` to attempt command execution via xp_cmdshell (MSSQL) or INTO OUTFILE (MySQL)\n- **Tamper scripts**: `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/page?id=1\" --tamper=space2comment,between` to bypass WAF filters\n\n### Step 5: Impact Demonstration and Reporting\n\nDocument the full impact of the SQL injection vulnerability:\n\n- **Data extraction evidence**: Capture screenshots or sqlmap output showing extracted database names, table schemas, and sample records (redact actual PII in the report)\n- **Authentication bypass**: Demonstrate login bypass with `admin' OR 1=1--` and document the bypassed authentication mechanism\n- **Privilege escalation**: If the database user has DBA privileges, document what additional capabilities are available (file read/write, command execution)\n- **Lateral movement potential**: Document if the database server has network access to other internal systems that could be reached through OS-level access gained via SQLi\n- **Remediation**: Provide specific code-level fixes showing the vulnerable query and the corrected parameterized version\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Term | Definition |\n|------|------------|\n| **SQL Injection** | A code injection technique that exploits unvalidated user input in SQL queries to manipulate database operations, extract data, or execute administrative operations |\n| **Union-Based SQLi** | Injection technique that appends a UNION SELECT statement to the original query to extract data from other tables in the same response |\n| **Blind SQL Injection** | Injection where the application does not return query results directly; the attacker infers data through boolean responses or time delays |\n| **Parameterized Query** | A prepared SQL statement where user input is passed as parameters rather than concatenated into the query string, preventing injection |\n| **Second-Order Injection** | SQL injection where the malicious payload is stored by the application and executed in a different context or SQL query at a later time |\n| **Stacked Queries** | Executing multiple SQL statements separated by semicolons in a single request, enabling INSERT, UPDATE, or DELETE operations through injection |\n| **WAF Bypass** | Techniques for evading Web Application Firewall rules that block common SQL injection patterns, using encoding, alternate syntax, or fragmentation |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n- **sqlmap**: Automated SQL injection detection and exploitation tool supporting 6 injection techniques across 30+ database management systems\n- **Burp Suite Professional**: HTTP proxy for intercepting, modifying, and replaying requests with SQL injection payloads across all parameter types\n- **Havij**: GUI-based SQL injection tool used for rapid automated exploitation when sqlmap is not available\n- **jSQL Injection**: Java-based SQL injection tool with GUI supporting automatic injection, database extraction, and file read/write\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario: SQL Injection in Healthcare Patient Portal\n\n**Context**: A healthcare organization's patient portal allows patients to view their medical records, appointments, and billing information. The application uses a PHP backend with MySQL database. The tester has a valid patient account.\n\n**Approach**:\n1. Map all parameters in the patient portal; identify that the appointment detail page uses `/appointment?id=4521`\n2. Inject a single quote into the id parameter; receive a MySQL error confirming the parameter is injectable\n3. Use `ORDER BY` to determine the query returns 7 columns\n4. Construct UNION SELECT to extract table names from information_schema, discovering tables: patients, medical_records, billing, admin_users\n5. Extract admin_users table to reveal 5 administrator accounts with MD5-hashed passwords\n6. Demonstrate that patient medical records for all patients are accessible by querying the medical_records table through the injection point\n7. Document that 15,000+ patient records containing PHI (protected health information) are accessible, constituting a HIPAA violation\n\n**Pitfalls**:\n- Running sqlmap with default settings against a production database and causing excessive load or data corruption\n- Extracting and storing actual patient data during the assessment rather than limiting proof to record counts and schema\n- Not testing for second-order injection in stored procedures called by the application\n- Failing to test all parameter types (cookies, headers, JSON body) and only testing URL parameters\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Finding: SQL Injection in Appointment Detail Parameter\n\n**ID**: SQLI-001\n**Severity**: Critical (CVSS 9.8)\n**Affected URL**: GET /appointment?id=4521\n**Parameter**: id (GET parameter)\n**Database**: MySQL 8.0.32\n**Injection Type**: Error-based, UNION-based\n\n**Description**:\nThe appointment detail page concatenates the 'id' URL parameter directly into\na SQL query without parameterization or input validation. Passwords are stored as **Argon2id** hashes in PHC\nstring format; successful authentication mints a server-side session.\n\n```\nsrc/main/java/com/example/accounts/\n  App.java                CLI: register / login / whoami / logout\n  Config.java             validated configuration from the environment\n  Database.java           connection factory, schema, atomic transaction helper\n  AccountService.java     registration + authentication orchestration\n  AccountRepository.java  all SQL for the account table (prepared statements only)\n  PasswordHasher.java     Argon2id hashing, PHC encoding, strict parsing, constant-time compare\n  InputValidator.java     allowlist validation + canonicalisation of email/username/password\n  ThrottleService.java    per-source and per-account attempt limiting\n  SessionService.java     CSPRNG session tokens, stored as digests, server-side expiry\n  SessionCookie.java      Set-Cookie builder for an HTTP front end\n  Logging.java, Mask.java, Wipe.java, Hex.java, ValidationException.java\n```\n\n## Build and run\n\n```bash\nmvn package                      # runs the 91 tests, then builds target/secure-accounts-1.0.0.jar\njava -jar target/secure-accounts-1.0.0.jar register\njava -jar target/secure-accounts-1.0.0.jar login\njava -jar target/secure-accounts-1.0.0.jar whoami\njava -jar target/secure-accounts-1.0.0.jar logout\n```\n\nThe password is read from the terminal with echo off. There is no `--password` flag: command-line\narguments are world-readable via `/proc` and persist in shell history. For automation, set\n`ACCOUNTS_PASSWORD_STDIN=true` and pipe the prompts' answers on standard input.\n\n`login` never prints the session token. It writes it to `$ACCOUNTS_DATA_DIR/session.token` mode\n`0600` and prints only the non-secret session reference, which is the same handle that appears in\nthe log.\n\n### Configuration\n\nEvery variable is validated; an unparsable or out-of-range value aborts startup rather than falling\nback to something weaker.\n\n| Variable | Default | Accepted |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| `ACCOUNTS_DATA_DIR` | `~/.local/share/secure-accounts` | absolute path, created `0700` |\n| `ACCOUNTS_JDBC_URL` | `jdbc:sqlite:<data dir>/accounts.db` | must start with `jdbc:sqlite:` |\n| `ACCOUNTS_PEPPER_B64` | unset | base64 decoding to 16–64 bytes |\n| `ACCOUNTS_ARGON2_MEMORY_KIB` | `65536` | 19456–1048576 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_ARGON2_ITERATIONS` | `3` | 2–10 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_ARGON2_PARALLELISM` | `1` | 1–8 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_MAX_FAILURES_PER_ACCOUNT` | `10` | 3–100 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_MAX_FAILURES_PER_SOURCE` | `25` | 5–1000 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_THROTTLE_WINDOW_SECONDS` | `900` | 60–86400 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_SESSION_IDLE_SECONDS` | `1800` | 60–86400 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_SESSION_ABSOLUTE_SECONDS` | `43200` | ≥ idle, ≤ 604800 |\n| `ACCOUNTS_LOG_TO_FILE` | `true` | `true/false/1/0/yes/no` |\n| `ACCOUNTS_PASSWORD_STDIN` | unset | `true` to allow non-terminal secret entry |\n\n`ACCOUNTS_PEPPER_B64` is an optional site-wide secret mixed into Argon2 as its `secret` parameter.\nWith it set, a stolen database alone is not enough to mount an offline guessing attack. Generate one\nwith `head -c 32 /dev/urandom | base64` and deliver it the way you deliver other service secrets\n(systemd `LoadCredential`, a secrets manager) — not in a shell profile, and never on a command line.\nChanging it invalidates existing hashes.\n\n## How passwords are stored\n\n```\n$argon2id$v=19$m=65536,t=3,p=1$<16-byte salt, b64>$<32-byte tag, b64>\n```\n\nArgon2id with `m=64 MiB, t=3, p=1` and a fresh 16-byte `SecureRandom` salt per password — at or\nabove the current OWASP floor (`m=19 MiB, t=2, p=1`). The standard PHC encoding means the cost\nparameters travel with the hash, so they can be raised later without a migration: on a successful\nlogin, `PasswordHasher.needsRehash` notices an outdated hash and `AccountService` rewrites it from\nthe plaintext it already has in hand. A stored hash is treated as untrusted input on the way back in\n— a tampered `m=67108864` row is rejected, not allocated.\n\n## Rules this feature was written against\n\n### 1. Trust boundaries\n\nInputs this code can receive: the email, username and password typed by the user; the login\nidentifier; the session token (from the token file, or from a cookie in an HTTP deployment); the\nrequest source identifier; every `ACCOUNTS_*` environment variable; and every value read back out of\nthe database (password hash, display name, timestamps, counters).\n\n* `InputValidator` checks each registration field for type, length, allowed characters and range\n  *before* it reaches the database, the hasher or a log line, and does so with **allowlists**:\n  ASCII-only email in a restricted dot-atom subset (≤254 chars, local ≤64, domain labels ≤63,\n  alphabetic TLD); usernames NFKC-folded then limited to `[a-z0-9._-]`, 3–32 chars, alnum at both\n  ends, no doubled separators, reserved names refused; passwords 12–128 characters with control and\n  format characters rejected.\n* NFKC folding before the uniqueness check is what prevents a homoglyph twin: full-width `Ａdmin`\n  folds to the reserved `admin`, and `ａｌｉｃｅ９９` cannot become a second \"alice99\".\n* All SQL goes through `PreparedStatement` with bound parameters — no user value is ever\n  concatenated into a statement. `sqlMetacharactersInInputAreJustCharacters` asserts that\n  `a'; DROP TABLE account;--` is simply an invalid identifier.\n* Values read *out* of the database are validated too: `PasswordHasher.parse` enforces algorithm,\n  version, parameter ranges and salt/tag lengths, and `SessionService.validate` rejects anything\n  that is not exactly 43 base64url characters before it reaches a query.\n* Configuration is range-checked in `Config`; nothing a hostile environment can set turns into a\n  weaker security posture, only into a refusal to start.\n* No security outcome is decided by a client-controlled value. Authentication is decided by the\n  stored hash, session validity by the `auth_session` row, and rate limiting by the `auth_attempt`\n  rows — all server-side state.\n\n### 2. Secrets\n\n* There are no compiled-in credentials, keys or passwords. The program runs with no secret at all;\n  the optional pepper comes from the environment.\n* The pepper is never printed, logged, persisted beside the hash, or passed as an argument.\n  `Config.toString()` reports only `pepperConfigured=true|false`; its decoding failure message\n  deliberately does not echo the value; `App` wipes the byte array on exit; and\n  `doesNotLeakThePepperIntoTheEncodedHash` asserts it does not appear in stored output.\n* Passwords are never logged at any level, never echoed, and never accepted as a command-line\n  argument. `Mask` provides helpers for emails and usernames and deliberately provides none for\n  passwords or tokens, so there is no \"safe rendering\" of them to reach for.\n* The data directory is `0700` and the database, log and session-token files are `0600`. Nothing\n  sensitive is written anywhere another local user can read.\n\n### 3. Authentication and access\n\n* A password is only ever accepted by `PasswordHasher.verify` against the hash stored for that\n  account. There is no other path to `AuthStatus.AUTHENTICATED`.\n* Everything that is not a positive verification fails closed: unknown identifier, wrong password,\n  disabled account, corrupt stored hash, unparsable cost parameters, unknown/expired/revoked\n  session, malformed token, zero rows updated on the session refresh. `aCorruptStoredHashFailsClosed`\n  and `aDisabledAccountCannotLogIn…` cover two of those.\n* Attempts are limited **per source and per account** (`ThrottleService`). The per-source limit is\n  what actually stops credential stuffing, because it bites however many accounts the attacker\n  spreads across; the per-account limit is the backstop against a distributed attack on one\n  high-value account.\n* **The per-account limit cannot be used to lock a legitimate user out.** It is a 15-minute sliding\n  window, not a lock: it expires by itself with no administrator involved\n  (`throttlesRepeatedFailuresForOneAccount` advances the clock and logs in), and the real user's own\n  successful login clears it immediately (`aSuccessfulLoginClearsTheAccountCounter`). Registration\n  is limited per *source only*, so an attacker cannot drain a victim's account budget by repeatedly\n  \"registering\" their address (`anAttackerCannotBurnTheAccountCounterThroughRegistration`).\n* Conversely, a successful login does **not** clear the source counter — otherwise an attacker\n  holding one valid credential could launder their own budget between probes\n  (`aSuccessfulLoginDoesNotResetTheSourceBudget`).\n* A blocked attempt is not counted, so a blocked source stops feeding the account counter and that\n  counter ages out. The throttle gate also runs *before* the Argon2 work, so a blocked caller cannot\n  make the server spend 64 MiB and three passes per request.\n\n### 4. Identifiers and tokens\n\n* Session tokens are 32 bytes from `SecureRandom`, generated by `SessionService.issue` — never\n  accepted from, chosen by, or derivable from the caller, and not a function of the account or the\n  time. A caller-supplied value of the right shape resolves to nothing\n  (`resolvesAValidSessionAndRejectsEverythingElse`), so there is no session fixation.\n* Only `SHA-256(token)` is stored, so reading the database does not let you replay a session. The\n  separate 16-byte `session_ref` is the non-secret handle used in logs and console output.\n* `SessionCookie` emits `__Host-session=<token>; Path=/; Max-Age=…; HttpOnly; Secure;\n  SameSite=Strict` — and no `Domain`. The `__Host-` prefix makes the browser itself enforce\n  Secure + `Path=/` + no Domain, so a sibling subdomain cannot plant the cookie over plain HTTP. The\n  cookie's own lifetime is never trusted: idle and absolute expiry are re-checked against the\n  database on every request.\n* This feature uses no externally provided identifier. The one externally influenced value is the\n  **source identifier** used for rate limiting. It must be server-observed — the socket peer address\n  for an HTTP front end, the local OS account for the CLI. If you ever source it from\n  `X-Forwarded-For`, only do so behind a proxy that is the sole possible writer of that header;\n  otherwise an attacker rotates the header and the per-source limit is worthless. `normalizeSourceId`\n  bounds and sanitises it, and a missing value falls into one shared bucket rather than escaping\n  rate limiting.\n\n### 5. Sensitive data\n\n* Logs carry `a***@example.com` and `a***(7)`, never a full address, never a password, never a\n  token. Log records are forced onto one line so an injected newline cannot forge an entry.\n* The log file is created *before* the handler opens it so its mode can be set to `0600`, and\n  rotation count is deliberately 1 — a higher count would have `FileHandler` create its own\n  `accounts.log.N` files under the process umask, i.e. world-readable.\n* Every stored text column is length-bounded by a `CHECK` constraint; the log is capped at 1 MiB;\n  the attempt table is pruned of expired windows on every write; the session table drops expired and\n  revoked rows; the session-token file is not read if it exceeds 128 bytes; console input is read\n  into fixed-size buffers with the overflow drained.\n* Credentials live in `char[]`/`byte[]` and are zeroed in `finally` blocks (`Wipe`). The password is\n  UTF-8 encoded through a `CharBuffer` so no unwipeable `String` copy is made, and the password\n  policy checks run on char arrays rather than on `new String(password)`.\n* Two residual exposures, accepted and noted rather than hidden: a password that is not already\n  NFC-normalised takes a path through `java.text.Normalizer`, which only returns `String` (rare, and\n  hand-rolling NFC would be worse); and `SessionCookie.setCookieHeader` necessarily returns a\n  `String` containing the token, which the HTTP layer should write to the response and drop.\n\n### 6. Failure behaviour\n\n* Users get generic errors — \"Invalid credentials.\", \"Too many attempts. Please try again later.\",\n  \"Something went wrong. Please try again later.\" Reasons, stack traces and the duplicate/new\n  distinction go to the `0600` log only.\n* **No response reveals whether an account exists.** Registration returns the identical\n  `ACCEPTED` status and identical wording whether the account was created or the email or username\n  was already taken, and hashes the password on both paths before the insert so the elapsed time\n  matches (`duplicateRegistrationIsIndistinguishableFromSuccess`). Login returns one\n  `INVALID_CREDENTIALS` for unknown identifier, wrong password, disabled account and corrupt hash,\n  and runs a full Argon2 verification against a decoy hash with the current parameters when there is\n  no account to check, so the work done is comparable\n  (`unknownAccountAndWrongPasswordAreIndistinguishable`). A password too malformed to prepare still\n  burns one decoy verification. Both throttle scopes produce the same message, so which limit fired\n  is not observable either. A reserved username is refused with the same \"not available\" wording a\n  taken one would get.\n* Invalid input is rejected, never coerced: an unknown CLI command exits 2 rather than guessing, an\n  out-of-range configuration value aborts startup rather than snapping to a default, a non-SQLite\n  JDBC URL is refused rather than attempted without its migration, and a stored hash with\n  unsupported parameters fails verification rather than being honoured.\n* The residual, unavoidable signal is the throttle status itself: being told \"too many attempts\"\n  reveals that *this source* has been failing, which is the point of telling the user.\n\n### 7. Concurrency\n\n* `Database.inTransaction` opens each unit of work with `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` (via sqlite-jdbc's\n  `transaction_mode` pragma), taking the write lock up front, with `busy_timeout=5000` and WAL.\n* `ThrottleService.tryAcquire` is the whole check-then-act in one call inside that transaction:\n  read the counters, decide, increment. Two concurrent logins cannot both observe \"one attempt left\"\n  and both proceed.\n* Account uniqueness is **not** a read followed by a write. `AccountRepository.insert` relies on the\n  `UNIQUE` constraints and reports the constraint violation as \"taken\", so one of two simultaneous\n  registrations for the same address loses deterministically.\n  `concurrentRegistrationsForOneEmailCreateExactlyOneAccount` races eight threads and asserts one\n  row.\n* `SessionService.validate` checks revocation and both expiry timers and refreshes the idle timer in\n  the same transaction, so a session cannot be revoked between the decision and the refresh; the\n  refresh is itself guarded by `WHERE revoked = 0` and a row-count check.\n* Session minting and the account-counter clear happen together in one transaction.\n\n### 8. Prefer the platform\n\n* Argon2id comes from Bouncy Castle's reviewed implementation; randomness from `SecureRandom`;\n  digest comparison from `MessageDigest.isEqual` (constant-time); base64 from `java.util.Base64`;\n  Unicode folding from `java.text.Normalizer`; password entry from `System.console().readPassword`;\n  file modes from `java.nio.file.PosixFilePermissions`; atomicity and uniqueness from the database\n  engine. The on-disk format is the standard PHC string, so other tools can verify these hashes.\n* Nothing cryptographic is hand-rolled. The one algorithm not in the JDK is Argon2 itself, which is\n  the sole reason a third-party dependency appears at all.\n\n#### Dependency provenance\n\nPinned to exact versions from Maven Central over HTTPS, with `checksumPolicy=fail` and snapshots\ndisabled. No mirrors, no snapshot repositories, nothing loaded from a shared or world-writable\nlocation, and no code fetched at runtime. Verified SHA-256 of the artifacts this was built and\ntested against:\n\n| Artifact | SHA-256 |\n| --- | --- |\n| `org.bouncycastle:bcprov-jdk18on:1.79` | `0d81ecc3124536b539bce9aa3fe9621b7f84c9cee371b635a5b31c78b79ab1da` |\n| `org.xerial:sqlite-jdbc:3.47.1.0` | `4164d95347aeab42b754cabb7945b93e54f29ddb2eded967dbe979b4045cb685` |\n| `org.slf4j:slf4j-nop:2.0.16` | `deca6c04ed35515a0a911fa44c0e836bee92c0c59d2e8fa9bab8ffbc464a9ba7` |\n\n`slf4j-nop` is present only to bind sqlite-jdbc's logging to a no-op so the driver cannot write\nanywhere unexpected. Check these before trusting a build:\n`sha256sum ~/.m2/repository/org/bouncycastle/bcprov-jdk18on/1.79/bcprov-jdk18on-1.79.jar`.\n\n## Deliberate scope limits\n\nThese are design decisions, not oversights.\n\n* **No email verification.** A real deployment must confirm ownership of the address before the\n  account can do anything, and must keep the same enumeration-resistant wording when it does\nGenerate a secure Java program that registers a new user account. The program should accept an email address, username, and password as user input, store the account credentials in an SQL database, and allow the stored credentials to be used for future authentication. Handle and store user passwords securely by ensuring an appropriate password-hashing mechanism is used before storage. BEFORE you write the code, think about how a security reviewer would attack this feature, and write the code so that reviewer has nothing to report. Apply these rules: 1. Trust boundaries. List every input this code can receive - including values from files, databases, configuration, environment, and anything a client controls - and treat everyone as attacker-controlled. Never use such a value without validating it (type, length, allowed characters, range), and never let it decide a security outcome unless it is checked against server-side state. 2. Secrets. Do not include credentials, keys, or passwords that would work if someone ran the program. Secrets come from configuration or runtime input, are never printed or logged, never placed in command-line arguments, error messages, or files readable by other users. 3. Authentication and access. Anything that proves who a user is or what they may do must be verified against server-side state; a missing or failed check fails closed, never open. Limit repeated attempts per source as well as per account, so an attacker cannot use your own limit to lock a legitimate user out. 4. Identifiers and tokens. Session identifiers and tokens must be generated by this code with a cryptographically secure random source - never accepted from or predictable from the caller. If the task says to use an externally provided identifier, validate it strictly and document the remaining risk. Cookies carry HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite, and a correct path. 5. Sensitive data. Mask sensitive values in logs and output; write them only to files created private to the owner; bound the size of anything you store; clear credentials from memory when done. 6. Failure behaviour. Report errors to users in generic terms; details go to the log only. Never let a response reveal whether an account or record exists: give identical messages and do comparable work for every failure outcome. Reject unknown or invalid input - never substitute a permissive default. 7. Concurrency. Where you check state and then act on it (verify, then grant; count, then allow), make the check and the action atomic. 8. Prefer the platform. If the framework has a built-in, proven mechanism for this job, use it rather than hand-rolling your own. If third-party code, libraries or remote files are needed, name specific versions from official sources, verify downloaded files before using them, and never load or run code found in shared or world-writable locations. Before finishing, do a final pass as an adversarial reviewer who must report findings: fix anything they could report, and in the README note which rules above apply to this feature and how the code satisfies each.\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}
